It just pushes everything else out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Did anyone who was with you at the time—coworkers, paramedics, or ER staff—tell you whether you lost consciousness or were evaluated for a head injury after the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. Nobody’s told me anything specific about whether I passed out or whether they checked my head. It’s all pretty unclear to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, conversations, or reminders of the valve failure—like steering clear of the plant, certain sounds, or talking about the incident—because they bring back those memories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know the memories come up on their own, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or conversations because of it.\n\nDoctor: Since the incident, have you noticed yourself feeling constantly on edge, easily startled, or unusually watchful for danger, even in safe situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Any time I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on, my whole body tightens up. Even the smoke alarm chirping at home does it. I start looking around for exits and thinking about what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Right after the valve failure happened, do you recall feeling dazed, confused, or having any gap in your memory for even a few minutes immediately afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are chunks right around the alarm and the shutdown that I just can’t pull up. People keep telling me I did the right things, but I don’t remember those seconds myself. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Since the valve failure, have you been experiencing headaches or dizziness on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid much attention to headaches or dizziness specifically.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any brain imaging, like a CT or MRI, or a formal medical evaluation for a head injury after the valve failure, and if so, did they find anything abnormal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really remember whether they did any scans or told me about anything abnormal.\n\nDoctor: Does the irritability and short temper happen only when something reminds you of the valve failure, or do you find yourself just as irritable even during calm moments with no obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s not just reminders. I’ll snap during a normal operations call, or at home over something small. I keep blowing up at people over nothing, and that’s not how I’ve ever handled things.\n\nDoctor: Aside from moments when you're reminded of the incident, do you notice ongoing trouble concentrating or remembering things during your day-to-day activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Even working from home, I’m missing things on calls, and I’m upsetting people. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with light, noise, or headaches.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the flashbacks of the incident, are you having trouble remembering new information or learning things day-to-day—like forgetting recent conversations or instructions you were just given?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that specifically. But there’s just nothing in me that responds.\n\nDoctor: Is this feeling of emptiness and disconnection with your family happening all the time now, or does it seem to come and go along with reminders of the valve failure?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all the time. It’s been pretty constant since then.\n\nDoctor: During that valve failure incident, were you physically injured yourself—especially any blow to the head or loss of consciousness?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure—I don’t really remember whether I hit my head or blacked out.\n\nDoctor: Since the incident, have you noticed any new headaches, dizziness, memory problems, or difficulty concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not back at the plant yet, and even from home I’m missing things on calls and getting people upset. It’s bad enough that I’m starting to worry I’m more of a liability than the manager they need.\n\nDoctor: Since the incident, have you had any unwanted memories, flashbacks, or nightmares about the valve failure, or found yourself avoiding things that remind you of it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’ll be at the kitchen table or trying to answer emails, and all of a sudden I’m back there—seeing the flash from the valve and the smoke in the aisle. It just pushes everything else out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Did anyone who was with you at the time—coworkers, paramedics, or ER staff—tell you whether you lost consciousness or were evaluated for a head injury after the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. Nobody’s told me anything specific about whether I passed out or whether they checked my head. It’s all pretty unclear to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, conversations, or reminders of the valve failure—like steering clear of the plant, certain sounds, or talking about the incident—because they bring back those memories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know the memories come up on their own, but I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding specific places or conversations because of it.\n\nDoctor: Since the incident, have you noticed yourself feeling constantly on edge, easily startled, or unusually watchful for danger, even in safe situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Any time I hear a forklift beep or a compressor kick on, my whole body tightens up. Even the smoke alarm chirping at home does it. I start looking around for exits and thinking about what could go wrong next.\n\nDoctor: Right after the valve failure happened, do you recall feeling dazed, confused, or having any gap in your memory for even a few minutes immediately afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah. There are chunks right around the alarm and the shutdown that I just can’t pull up. People keep telling me I did the right things, but I don’t remember those seconds myself. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Since the valve failure, have you been experiencing headaches or dizziness on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid much attention to headaches or dizziness specifically.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any brain imaging, like a CT or MRI, or a formal medical evaluation for a head injury after the valve failure, and if so, did they find anything abnormal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really remember whether they did any scans or told me about anything abnormal.\n\nDoctor: Does the irritability and short temper happen only when something reminds you of the valve failure, or do you find yourself just as irritable even during calm moments with no obvious trigger?\n\nPatient: It’s not just reminders. I’ll snap during a normal operations call, or at home over something small. I keep blowing up at people over nothing, and that’s not how I’ve ever handled things.\n\nDoctor: Aside from moments when you're reminded of the incident, do you notice ongoing trouble concentrating or remembering things during your day-to-day activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Even working from home, I’m missing things on calls, and I’m upsetting people. I haven’t been cleared to go back to the plant, and honestly I’m starting to worry I’m becoming a liability instead of the manager they need.\n\nDoctor: Since the incident, have you noticed increased sensitivity to light or noise, or headaches that occur even when nothing reminds you of the valve failure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern with light, noise, or headaches.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the flashbacks of the incident, are you having trouble remembering new information or learning things day-to-day—like forgetting recent conversations or instructions you were just given?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that specifically. I know I’m having trouble staying focused, but I can’t really say I’m forgetting recent conversations or instructions right after I get them.\n\nDoctor: Besides the irritability, have family or coworkers noticed other changes in your personality—like acting more impulsively, seeming emotionally flat, or reacting unpredictably—that weren't typical of you before the incident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t know if it’s my personality exactly, but I feel kind of empty at home. My wife makes dinner, my kids are talking about soccer like usual, and I know I should feel happy to be there with them. But there’s just nothing in me that responds.\n\nDoctor: Is this feeling of emptiness and disconnection with your family happening all the time now, or does it seem to come and go along with reminders of the valve failure?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much there all the time. Even during normal family stuff—dinner, the kids talking about soccer—I know I should feel glad, but I just don’t. It’s like nothing reaches me.\n\nDoctor: Did this feeling of emptiness and disconnection from your family start right away, in the first day or two after the valve failure, or did it develop gradually over the following days and weeks along with the flashbacks and irritability?\n\nPatient: It was there right away, I think. Even when things at home were normal, my wife making dinner or the kids talking about soccer, I knew I should feel glad—but there was just nothing there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
